# Geriatrics MCQs – Social Determinants, Caregiver Burden, Housing, Nursing Home vs Community Care

**Instructions:** Choose the best answer for each question.

---

1. A 79-year-old female named Catherine with moderate dementia has been cared for at home by her spouse for the past 3 years. The spouse reports feeling overwhelmed, missing work, and having no time for personal activities. The clinician administers the Zarit Burden Interview (ZBI), and the spouse scores 58. Which of the following is the most appropriate interpretation and next step?
   - A) A ZBI score ≥ 21 indicates significant caregiver burden; refer the caregiver to a social worker, recommend a caregiver support group, arrange respite care services, and screen the caregiver for depression and burnout
   - B) The ZBI is used exclusively to assess the patient's cognitive decline; repeat the Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE) to evaluate the patient's disease progression
   - C) A ZBI score in this range mandates immediate involuntary psychiatric hospitalization of the caregiver for acute burnout syndrome
   - D) A ZBI score of this level is within normal limits for caregivers; reassure the spouse that these feelings are completely expected and require no intervention
   - E) Advise the caregiver that their only legal option is to continue providing full-time home care without institutional support until the patient dies
   - **Answer:** A

   - E) A ZBI score of this level is within normal limits for caregivers; reassure the spouse that these feelings are completely expected and require no intervention
   - **Answer:** B

11. A 85-year-old female named Edith with advanced Alzheimer's disease is brought to the clinic by her grandson, who has been the sole caregiver for 2 years. The clinician notices that the grandson appears tearful, disheveled, and states, 'I haven't slept in days, I've lost 18 pounds in a month, and I feel hopeless.' The clinician administers the PHQ-9 to the caregiver, and the score is 16. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate clinical response?
   - A) Tell the caregiver that mild sadness is a normal part of the caregiving role and prescribe a 2-week course of diphenhydramine to help them sleep
   - B) Immediately place the patient in a nursing home without discussing the family's preferences, as this is the most efficient way to resolve the caregiver's stress
   - C) Recognize that the caregiver is experiencing major depressive disorder; refer immediately for psychiatric evaluation and treatment, arrange emergency respite care for the patient, and conduct a safety assessment including suicide risk screening
   - D) Advise the caregiver to take a one-day vacation from their responsibilities, then return to full caregiving duties the following day
   - E) Document the caregiver's score in the patient's medical record as evidence of secondary self-neglect and file a report with Adult Protective Services
   - **Answer:** C

21. A 67-year-old female named Florence with moderate dementia lives at home. Her adult son reports severe burnout and states they desperately need a 2-week break to attend to their own health. The patient is medically stable with no acute illness but requires assistance with all 6 basic activities of daily living (ADLs). The family asks about care options during this period. Which of the following represents the most appropriate form of 'respite care' for this situation?
   - A) Advise the adult son that respite care is only covered by private insurance and is not available through Medicare, Medicaid, or community programs
   - B) Suggest that the family reduce the patient's caloric intake to induce mild sedation and reduce the caregiver's supervision burden
   - C) Instruct the caregiver to leave the patient home alone with daily phone check-ins, as the patient is medically stable
   - D) Recommend that the patient be admitted to the hospital for a 2-week 'social admission' while the caregiver rests
   - E) Arrange a short-term temporary placement in a skilled nursing facility (SNF) or a specialized dementia respite program, which provides supervised care for the patient while the caregiver takes a planned break
   - **Answer:** E

   - D) Arrange a short-term temporary placement in a skilled nursing facility (SNF) or a specialized dementia respite program, which provides supervised care for the patient while the caregiver takes a planned break
   - E) Suggest that the family reduce the patient's caloric intake to induce mild sedation and reduce the caregiver's supervision burden
   - **Answer:** D

30. A 79-year-old female named Evelyn with moderate dementia lives at home. Her daughter reports severe burnout and states they desperately need a 2-week break to attend to their own health. The patient is medically stable with no acute illness but requires assistance with all 6 basic activities of daily living (ADLs). The family asks about care options during this period. Which of the following represents the most appropriate form of 'respite care' for this situation?
   - A) Recommend that the patient be admitted to the hospital for a 2-week 'social admission' while the caregiver rests
   - B) Arrange a short-term temporary placement in a skilled nursing facility (SNF) or a specialized dementia respite program, which provides supervised care for the patient while the caregiver takes a planned break
   - C) Suggest that the family reduce the patient's caloric intake to induce mild sedation and reduce the caregiver's supervision burden
   - D) Instruct the caregiver to leave the patient home alone with daily phone check-ins, as the patient is medically stable
   - E) Advise the daughter that respite care is only covered by private insurance and is not available through Medicare, Medicaid, or community programs
   - **Answer:** B

31. A 85-year-old male named Donald with mild-to-moderate dementia lives at home with his working granddaughter. The granddaughter must work 14 hours per day and cannot provide daytime supervision. The patient wanders, forgets to eat, and has had two minor kitchen injuries. The patient remains ambulatory, social, and does not have complex medical needs requiring continuous nursing care. Which of the following is the most appropriate community-based care recommendation?
   - A) Prescribe a daily dose of lorazepam 2 mg to chemically suppress the wandering behavior during the caregiver's working hours
   - B) Arrange immediate permanent placement in a locked memory care nursing facility, as any degree of wandering requires 24-hour institutionalization
   - C) Enroll the patient in an Adult Day Health Program (ADHP), which provides structured daytime supervision, cognitive stimulation, meals, socialization, and safety monitoring while allowing the patient to return home in the evening
   - D) Recommend the patient wear a medical alert necklace and be left home alone with the stove disconnected as a safety measure
   - E) Advise the granddaughter to quit their job and provide full-time home care, as this is the most appropriate standard of care for patients with wandering behaviors
   - **Answer:** C

41. A 67-year-old female named Esther with severe dementia is cared for at home. You learn that the primary caregiver is the patient's 6-year-old grandson, who is also managing their own chronic conditions including Type 2 diabetes and hypertension. The grandson reports missing their own medical appointments and medications. Which of the following describes this phenomenon and the most appropriate clinical action?
   - A) The caregiver's missed appointments are solely the responsibility of their own primary care physician and do not fall within the scope of the geriatrician's clinical duties
   - B) File a report with Adult Protective Services (APS) immediately, as the caregiver's health management failure constitutes medical neglect of the patient
   - C) Advise the caregiver to manage their own health conditions using over-the-counter medications to avoid the time burden of medical appointments
   - D) This represents 'caregiver role strain,' where the caregiver's own health is compromised by caregiving demands; the clinician must assess both patient and caregiver needs, connect the caregiver to community support services, and ensure the caregiver maintains their own medical care
   - E) Recommend that both the patient and the caregiver be admitted to a skilled nursing facility simultaneously to provide coordinated care for both
   - **Answer:** D

51. A 87-year-old female named Ruby is dually eligible for Medicare and Medicaid. She has multiple chronic conditions (diabetes, COPD, mild dementia), requires assistance with 4 ADLs, and meets nursing home level of care criteria. However, Ruby and her family strongly prefer to remain in the community. A social worker mentions a program that provides comprehensive medical, social, and supportive services in a community setting. Which program is being described?
   - A) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit, which provides a single-day comprehensive health assessment but does not include ongoing community-based services
   - B) Medicare Part D Extra Help (Low Income Subsidy), which provides prescription drug cost assistance for low-income Medicare beneficiaries
   - C) Program of All-inclusive Care for the Elderly (PACE), which provides comprehensive interdisciplinary team-based care (medical, social, rehabilitative, and supportive services) through an adult day health center to nursing home-eligible individuals who choose to remain in the community
   - D) Medicaid Managed Long-Term Services and Supports (MLTSS), which exclusively provides nursing home care and excludes all community-based alternatives
   - E) The Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP), which provides monthly food vouchers for older adults with nutritional risk factors
   - **Answer:** C

   - D) Medicaid Managed Long-Term Services and Supports (MLTSS), which exclusively provides nursing home care and excludes all community-based alternatives
   - E) Program of All-inclusive Care for the Elderly (PACE), which provides comprehensive interdisciplinary team-based care (medical, social, rehabilitative, and supportive services) through an adult day health center to nursing home-eligible individuals who choose to remain in the community
   - **Answer:** E

61. A 77-year-old male named Albert is discharged home after a hip fracture repair. He needs assistance with bathing, dressing, and meal preparation (personal care ADLs), but has no wound care, IV therapy, or skilled therapy needs. The family asks what type of home-based assistance is most appropriate. Which of the following best describes the correct service?
   - A) A home health aide (HHA) or personal care aide (PCA), who provides non-skilled, custodial assistance with personal care activities of daily living (ADLs) such as bathing, dressing, grooming, and meal preparation
   - B) A Medicare-certified home health agency providing skilled nursing visits, as Medicare Part A covers all home assistance services for patients discharged from the hospital
   - C) A licensed clinical social worker (LCSW) for daily home visits to provide psychotherapy during the post-discharge recovery period
   - D) A durable medical equipment (DME) supplier to provide a hospital-grade bed and mechanical lifting device as the primary service
   - E) A hospice home care team, which is appropriate for any patient who is discharged from the hospital with functional limitations
   - **Answer:** A

71. A 93-year-old male named Clarence with advanced dementia is cared for at home by his paid caregiver. During a home health visit, the nurse notes the patient appears poorly groomed and has lost 18 lbs. The paid caregiver states: 'Some days I think about just disappearing or ending things for both of us so we can both be at peace.' Which of the following is the most critical immediate clinical action?
   - A) Advise the family to reduce the patient's food intake to minimize caregiver preparation burden, then reassess in 3 months
   - B) Reassure the caregiver that such thoughts are universally experienced by all long-term caregivers and require no immediate clinical response
   - C) Document the caregiver's statement as a metaphorical expression of frustration and take no further action at this visit
   - D) Recognize this as a potential caregiver-patient violence/homicide-suicide risk; immediately assess lethality, ensure the patient's safety, involve crisis mental health services for the caregiver, and report to Adult Protective Services (APS)
   - E) Prescribe the caregiver a one-week supply of zolpidem 5 mg to improve sleep and schedule a follow-up in 6 weeks
   - **Answer:** D

81. A 83-year-old female named Martha with moderate dementia is cared for at home by her grandson. The family has no private insurance for home care services and asks the clinician what publicly funded support programs exist for family caregivers. Which of the following accurately describes a federally funded program specifically designed to support informal family caregivers of older adults?
   - A) The American Geriatrics Society (AGS) operates a national network of free 24/7 respite care centers funded entirely by private donations
   - B) Social Security Disability Insurance (SSDI) automatically reimburses caregivers at a rate of $15 per hour for all hours spent providing care to a disabled family member
   - C) The National Family Caregiver Support Program (NFCSP), authorized under Title III-E of the Older Americans Act, provides caregivers with information, assistance, counseling, respite care, and supplemental services through local Area Agencies on Aging (AAA)
   - D) The Patient Self-Determination Act mandates that all hospitals provide 40 hours of free respite care per month to family caregivers of hospitalized patients
   - E) Medicare Part B's 'Caregiver Benefit' provides direct monthly payments to family members who care for Medicare beneficiaries at home as a substitute for institutional care
   - **Answer:** C

88. A 77-year-old male named Harry with moderate dementia is cared for at home by his adult son. The family has no private insurance for home care services and asks the clinician what publicly funded support programs exist for family caregivers. Which of the following accurately describes a federally funded program specifically designed to support informal family caregivers of older adults?
   - A) The National Family Caregiver Support Program (NFCSP), authorized under Title III-E of the Older Americans Act, provides caregivers with information, assistance, counseling, respite care, and supplemental services through local Area Agencies on Aging (AAA)
   - B) The Patient Self-Determination Act mandates that all hospitals provide 40 hours of free respite care per month to family caregivers of hospitalized patients
   - C) The American Geriatrics Society (AGS) operates a national network of free 24/7 respite care centers funded entirely by private donations
   - D) Social Security Disability Insurance (SSDI) automatically reimburses caregivers at a rate of $15 per hour for all hours spent providing care to a disabled family member
   - E) Medicare Part B's 'Caregiver Benefit' provides direct monthly payments to family members who care for Medicare beneficiaries at home as a substitute for institutional care
   - **Answer:** A

91. A 91-year-old male named Clarence with advanced dementia requires 24-hour supervision. His daughter has quit their job to provide full-time care and the family's income has dropped to $800/month Social Security. The daughter reports they cannot afford adult diapers, nutritional supplements, or medication copays. What is the most appropriate primary clinical intervention to address this financial crisis?
   - A) Refer to a licensed social worker for a comprehensive financial eligibility assessment including Medicaid (for long-term services), Supplemental Security Income (SSI), Medicare Extra Help, the Low Income Home Energy Assistance Program (LIHEAP), and local Area Agency on Aging benefits counseling
   - B) Advise the family to take out a second mortgage on their home to fund the care costs, as this is the standard expected financial sacrifice for long-term caregiving
   - C) Immediately place the patient in a nursing home as the sole solution to the financial crisis without assessing eligibility for community-based Medicaid programs
   - D) Tell the family that until they spend down all assets to Medicaid eligibility thresholds, no public assistance programs are available to them
   - E) Prescribe generic versions of all medications only and discharge the family with no further social support
   - **Answer:** A

101. A 73-year-old female named Florence has just been diagnosed with mild Alzheimer's disease. Her grandson asks the clinician, 'What should we plan for over the next few years?' Which of the following represents the most appropriate and comprehensive response regarding the caregiver trajectory in Alzheimer's disease?
   - A) Tell the grandson that home care for Alzheimer's is unsustainable beyond 6 months and immediate nursing home placement is the clinically recommended option
   - B) Provide anticipatory guidance: caregiving demands will progressively increase as the disease advances through mild, moderate, and severe stages; early-stage planning should include legal/financial preparations (power of attorney, advance directives), identifying future care resources, caregiver support enrollment, and discussing the likely eventual need for residential care
   - C) Focus exclusively on pharmacological management of the patient's symptoms and defer all social planning discussions to a separate social work appointment next year
   - D) Advise the family to purchase a long-term care insurance policy immediately, as this is the only reliable funding source for future dementia care
   - E) Reassure the caregiver that Alzheimer's disease has a very gradual course requiring minimal caregiving adjustments over time, and no long-term planning is necessary at this early stage
   - **Answer:** B

111. A 75-year-old female named Lillian with advanced dementia is cared for exclusively by family members in a multigenerational household. The family is from a cultural background in which nursing home placement is considered deeply stigmatizing and a betrayal of family duty. Despite the patient's increasing care needs, the family categorically refuses institutional care and is struggling significantly. Which of the following represents the most culturally competent clinical approach?
   - A) Arrange nursing home placement without the family's consent as the clinician knows what is medically best, regardless of patient and family values
   - B) Discharge the patient from geriatric care and refer exclusively to a social worker, as cultural issues are outside the scope of geriatric medicine
   - C) Respect the family's cultural values while honestly discussing the increasing care demands; collaboratively explore culturally acceptable alternatives such as in-home skilled nursing, PACE, culturally aligned adult day programs, and offer caregiver burnout resources without imposing institutional placement
   - D) Inform the family that their cultural beliefs are clinically inappropriate and that nursing home placement is legally mandated once a patient requires assistance with more than three ADLs
   - E) File a report with Adult Protective Services for medical neglect because the family is refusing institutional care
   - **Answer:** C

Which of the following represents the most culturally competent clinical approach?
   - A) Arrange nursing home placement without the family's consent as the clinician knows what is medically best, regardless of patient and family values
   - B) Respect the family's cultural values while honestly discussing the increasing care demands; collaboratively explore culturally acceptable alternatives such as in-home skilled nursing, PACE, culturally aligned adult day programs, and offer caregiver burnout resources without imposing institutional placement
   - C) Inform the family that their cultural beliefs are clinically inappropriate and that nursing home placement is legally mandated once a patient requires assistance with more than three ADLs
   - D) File a report with Adult Protective Services for medical neglect because the family is refusing institutional care
   - E) Discharge the patient from geriatric care and refer exclusively to a social worker, as cultural issues are outside the scope of geriatric medicine
   - **Answer:** B

121. A 77-year-old female named Elizabeth with mild cognitive impairment and a recent fall is being discharged home. A home safety evaluation is ordered. Which of the following home modifications has the strongest evidence for reducing falls in community-dwelling older adults?
   - A) Placing the patient in physical restraints in a recliner chair during all waking hours to prevent unassisted ambulation
   - B) Moving the patient to the ground floor only while installing wall-to-wall padded flooring throughout the entire home
   - C) Installation of grab bars in the bathroom (tub, shower, toilet), removal of loose throw rugs, adequate night lighting in hallways and bathrooms, securing loose electrical cords, and referral to occupational therapy for a formal home safety assessment
   - D) Purchasing an expensive commercial smart-home fall detection system as the primary fall prevention strategy, without addressing structural home hazards
   - E) Prescribing a wheelchair for all indoor ambulation to eliminate the risk of falls from walking
   - **Answer:** C

131. A 67-year-old male named Clarence with heart failure is seen in the clinic in late July. He lives alone in a third-floor apartment with no air conditioning. His core body temperature is 38.8°C, he is mildly confused, and his medications include a loop diuretic and a beta-blocker. He income is $720/month fixed income. Which of the following is the most appropriate immediate and coordinated clinical response?
   - A) Admit to the hospital solely for the purpose of providing a cool environment and discharge after 24 hours without addressing the underlying housing hazard
   - B) Discharge the patient home immediately with strict instructions to open windows and drink 8 glasses of water daily, as this is sufficient to prevent hyperthermia
   - C) Prescribe a higher dose of loop diuretics to counteract the fluid shift caused by heat exposure and plan routine follow-up in 1 month
   - D) Tell the patient that air conditioning is a luxury item and their housing arrangement requires no medical intervention
   - E) Address the immediate heat-related illness medically; provide cooling measures and IV hydration if indicated; connect the patient with LIHEAP (Low Income Home Energy Assistance Program) for air conditioning unit funding; explore emergency housing options or 'cooling center' access; assess medication safety in the heat context
   - **Answer:** E

141. A 67-year-old female named Grace with mild cognitive impairment lives alone in a rural area. She has no family nearby, rarely leaves the home, has not seen a friend in 3 months, and scores positively on the UCLA Loneliness Scale. She primary care clinician is concerned about her safety and well-being. Which of the following represents the most comprehensive clinical approach to address social isolation in this older adult?
   - A) Prescribe a high-dose benzodiazepine to reduce the patient's anxiety associated with social isolation
   - B) Discharge the patient from geriatric care as social isolation is a personal lifestyle choice outside the scope of medical practice
   - C) Recommend the patient immediately move to a large urban area where social opportunities are more available
   - D) Advise the patient to join a social media platform, as this is the primary evidence-based intervention for social isolation in older adults
   - E) Screen formally for loneliness and social isolation; connect with the Area Agency on Aging for services (Meals on Wheels, telephone reassurance, friendly visitor programs, senior center transportation); evaluate for depression; and consider telehealth check-ins to maintain medical oversight
   - **Answer:** E

148. A 97-year-old male named Carl with mild cognitive impairment lives alone in a rural area. He has no family nearby, rarely leaves the home, has not seen a friend in 12 months, and scores positively on the UCLA Loneliness Scale. He primary care clinician is concerned about his safety and well-being. Which of the following represents the most comprehensive clinical approach to address social isolation in this older adult?
   - A) Prescribe a high-dose benzodiazepine to reduce the patient's anxiety associated with social isolation
   - B) Screen formally for loneliness and social isolation; connect with the Area Agency on Aging for services (Meals on Wheels, telephone reassurance, friendly visitor programs, senior center transportation); evaluate for depression; and consider telehealth check-ins to maintain medical oversight
   - C) Discharge the patient from geriatric care as social isolation is a personal lifestyle choice outside the scope of medical practice
   - D) Recommend the patient immediately move to a large urban area where social opportunities are more available
   - E) Advise the patient to join a social media platform, as this is the primary evidence-based intervention for social isolation in older adults
   - **Answer:** B

151. A 83-year-old female named Elizabeth is brought to the emergency department by police after being found confused and sleeping in a park. She has a history of alcohol use disorder, hypertension, and is found to be in early-moderate cognitive decline on bedside testing. She states she has been without stable housing for 3 months. Which of the following represents the most comprehensive approach to this patient's care?
   - A) Discharge the patient from the emergency department with a bus token and a list of local churches, as housing is not a medical issue
   - B) Prescribe naltrexone for alcohol use disorder and arrange a clinic follow-up appointment only, without addressing the patient's housing crisis
   - C) Medically stabilize the patient; arrange a social work consultation for Housing First program referral, substance use disorder treatment, Medicaid enrollment, and connection to supportive housing with case management services; perform a formal cognitive and capacity assessment
   - D) Advise the patient to apply for Social Security Disability Insurance (SSDI) and wait 2 years for Medicare eligibility before receiving any further care
   - E) Admit the patient to a locked psychiatric facility for indefinite care due to cognitive impairment combined with homelessness
   - **Answer:** C

161. In Texas, a public health official named Lois is presenting a community initiative to a group of geriatricians. The initiative targets a large urban apartment complex where over 60% of residents are adults ≥ 65 years old who have 'aged in place' in their original apartments over several decades. Many residents have multiple chronic conditions, functional impairments, and limited social support networks, but strongly prefer to remain in their homes. Which type of community-based intervention model is most specifically designed for this type of naturally occurring aging community?
   - A) A Medicare Annual Wellness Visit campaign, which provides a single annual assessment but no ongoing community-based services
   - B) A Naturally Occurring Retirement Community (NORC) Supportive Service Program (NORC-SSP), which brings coordinated health, social, and supportive services directly into residential buildings where a large proportion of residents are older adults
   - C) The State Children's Health Insurance Program (SCHIP), which is exclusively designed for pediatric populations and has no relevance to older adult housing
   - D) A hospital-based acute care for elders (ACE) unit, which is limited to inpatient hospitalized patients and is not applicable to community settings
   - E) A state-mandated mass relocation program to move all residents with ADL limitations to skilled nursing facilities
   - **Answer:** B

171. A 73-year-old male named Bernard with moderate osteoarthritis and mild balance impairment is planning to remodel his home to enable safe aging in place for the next decade. His occupational therapist recommends incorporating 'universal design' principles. Which of the following best describes the concept of universal design in the context of aging-in-place home modifications?
   - A) Universal design refers exclusively to the installation of overhead patient-lift systems (Hoyer lifts) in every room of the home
   - B) Universal design requires replacing all existing home appliances with hospital-grade medical equipment to create a clinical care environment at home
   - C) Universal design refers to the design of environments and products that are usable by all people to the greatest extent possible without adaptation, including wider doorways for wheelchairs, zero-step entries, lever-style door handles, curbless showers, and varied height countertops
   - D) Universal design requires that the home be rebuilt from the foundation using only government-approved building materials rated for geriatric occupancy
   - E) Universal design means that all home modifications must be pre-approved by Medicare before they can be considered medically legitimate
   - **Answer:** C

   - D) Universal design refers to the design of environments and products that are usable by all people to the greatest extent possible without adaptation, including wider doorways for wheelchairs, zero-step entries, lever-style door handles, curbless showers, and varied height countertops
   - E) Universal design requires that the home be rebuilt from the foundation using only government-approved building materials rated for geriatric occupancy
   - **Answer:** D

181. A 69-year-old female named Martha lives in a rundown studio apartment and pays 80% of her $720/month fixed income in rent, leaving insufficient money for food and medications. She is functionally independent with no ADL limitations. Her clinician wants to explore affordable, safe housing alternatives. Which of the following federal housing programs is specifically designed to provide subsidized, supportive housing for very low-income older adults who are functionally independent?
   - A) Medicaid's Institutional Care Program, which provides housing only in certified nursing facilities and is not applicable to independent community-dwelling older adults
   - B) The Veterans Affairs Aid and Attendance Benefit, which is exclusively available to eligible veterans and their spouses and is not applicable to the general older adult population
   - C) The Section 8 Housing Choice Voucher Program, which exclusively serves families with children under 18 years old and explicitly excludes older adult applicants
   - D) The Medicare Part A institutional care benefit, which covers permanent residential living in skilled nursing facilities for all low-income Medicare beneficiaries
   - E) HUD Section 202 Supportive Housing for the Elderly Program, which provides federally subsidized housing units with rents capped at 30% of income, along with access to supportive services coordination for functionally independent low-income older adults
   - **Answer:** E

191. A social worker reports to a geriatrician that a 79-year-old female named Alice with mild dementia is living in a small, unlicensed boarding home with 5 other older adults. The home is run by a private individual who charges monthly fees for room, board, and minimal assistance. There are no licensed nursing staff, no medication management policies, and no oversight by a state regulatory agency. What is the primary clinical and ethical concern in this situation?
   - A) Unlicensed board-and-care homes exist in a significant regulatory gap; residents are at high risk for neglect, financial exploitation, substandard care, and safety hazards; the clinician should facilitate a formal home safety and welfare assessment, report concerns to Adult Protective Services (APS), and explore licensed residential alternatives
   - B) Unlicensed facilities are not subject to any reporting obligations, and clinicians should not involve themselves in patients' private housing arrangements
   - C) The patient should be immediately arrested for illegal habitation in an unlicensed facility
   - D) This arrangement is optimal because the patient is receiving personalized care in a small-group home setting and should be encouraged to sign a long-term lease immediately
   - E) The home operator should be immediately licensed retroactively as a nurse practitioner to legitimize the care provided
   - **Answer:** A

201. A 87-year-old male named Henry requires assistance with 4 ADLs (bathing, dressing, meals, toileting), has mild dementia with no behavioral disturbances, no complex medical needs, no IV therapy, and no daily skilled nursing requirements. He family is exploring long-term residential care options. Which of the following care settings is most appropriate for this patient's current level of need?
   - A) Independent living retirement community, which requires residents to manage all ADLs independently without assistance and is therefore not appropriate for this patient
   - B) Inpatient psychiatric hospital, which is appropriate because mild dementia with ADL limitations is classified as a psychiatric emergency requiring inpatient stabilization
   - C) Assisted living facility (ALF), which provides 24-hour supervision, personal care (ADL assistance), meals, housekeeping, and social programming for individuals who need help with ADLs but do not require continuous skilled nursing care
   - D) Acute rehabilitation hospital, which is designed for patients requiring intensive physical and occupational therapy (minimum 3 hours/day) following an acute medical event
   - E) Skilled nursing facility (SNF), which is specifically indicated for patients requiring daily skilled nursing care (wound care, IV medications, complex rehabilitation) and would be over-provision for this patient's current needs
   - **Answer:** C

   - A) Inpatient psychiatric hospital, which is appropriate because mild dementia with ADL limitations is classified as a psychiatric emergency requiring inpatient stabilization
   - B) Acute rehabilitation hospital, which is designed for patients requiring intensive physical and occupational therapy (minimum 3 hours/day) following an acute medical event
   - C) Independent living retirement community, which requires residents to manage all ADLs independently without assistance and is therefore not appropriate for this patient
   - D) Assisted living facility (ALF), which provides 24-hour supervision, personal care (ADL assistance), meals, housekeeping, and social programming for individuals who need help with ADLs but do not require continuous skilled nursing care
   - E) Skilled nursing facility (SNF), which is specifically indicated for patients requiring daily skilled nursing care (wound care, IV medications, complex rehabilitation) and would be over-provision for this patient's current needs
   - **Answer:** D

211. A 87-year-old male named Albert requires permanent nursing home placement. He has $180,000 in savings and a monthly Social Security income of $880/month Social Security Disability. He family asks how nursing home care will be financed and when public assistance becomes available. Which of the following most accurately describes how nursing home care is typically financed in the United States?
   - A) Medicare Part B covers all nursing home costs permanently without any duration limit, asset requirements, or spend-down obligations
   - B) Nursing home care is completely free of charge for all Americans aged ≥ 65 years old as a standard Medicare benefit with no limitations or eligibility requirements
   - C) Private health insurance (not Medicare or Medicaid) is the primary payer for all long-term nursing home care in the United States
   - D) Nursing home care is initially financed by Medicare Part A (for post-acute skilled care only, limited to 100 days), then private pay using personal assets, and finally by Medicaid once the individual has spent down assets to the state-specified threshold (typically $2,000 for an individual); Medicaid then covers long-term custodial nursing home costs
   - E) Social Security income alone is sufficient to cover all nursing home costs, and no additional private payment or Medicaid enrollment is required
   - **Answer:** D

221. A 73-year-old male named Thomas with advanced dementia and total ADL dependence is a long-term resident of a skilled nursing facility. During a clinical visit, you review the facility's quality indicators and note a high rate of new pressure injuries, frequent use of physical restraints, a high incidence of urinary tract infections, and multiple hospitalizations in the past quarter. What is the most appropriate clinical and advocacy response?
   - A) These represent evidence of poor nursing home quality; discuss findings with the nursing home medical director and director of nursing, report concerns to the State Survey Agency (or long-term care ombudsman), review individual patient care plans, and advocate for evidence-based care improvements
   - B) Immediately transfer all patients to other facilities without completing a formal investigation, as this is the fastest way to protect patients
   - C) Accept these indicators as normal and expected for an advanced dementia population; document them in the chart without any further advocacy
   - D) Reassure the family that federal regulations do not apply to private-pay nursing home residents, and therefore quality indicators are irrelevant for this patient
   - E) Report the findings exclusively to the nursing home's marketing department for internal quality improvement without involving any external regulatory bodies
   - **Answer:** A

231. A 89-year-old female named Shirley with severe dementia is cared for at home by her exhausted nephew. Despite home health aide services 12 hours/day, the patient wanders nocturnally, has severe sundowning with agitation requiring physical intervention, is incontinent of bowel and bladder, and has developed a Stage III sacral pressure injury. The nephew has had multiple falls while trying to reposition the patient at night. Which of the following represents the most appropriate clinical recommendation?
   - A) Prescribe high-dose quetiapine to eliminate all sundowning behaviors and continue indefinite home care without reassessment
   - B) Transfer the patient to an acute psychiatric inpatient unit for permanent long-term management of behavioral symptoms of dementia
   - C) Recommend the nephew purchase a hospital-style bed with four-point restraints and hire an additional uncertified family friend for nighttime repositioning
   - D) Continue home care at its current level with no changes, as families must never be recommended nursing home placement by a physician
   - E) The patient's care needs (nocturnal wandering, severe BPSD requiring physical intervention, total incontinence, Stage III pressure injury, caregiver injury risk) now exceed what can be safely managed at home; nursing home placement in a secure memory care unit with 24-hour skilled nursing is clinically indicated
   - **Answer:** E

   - C) The patient's care needs (nocturnal wandering, severe BPSD requiring physical intervention, total incontinence, Stage III pressure injury, caregiver injury risk) now exceed what can be safely managed at home; nursing home placement in a secure memory care unit with 24-hour skilled nursing is clinically indicated
   - D) Recommend the daughter purchase a hospital-style bed with four-point restraints and hire an additional uncertified family friend for nighttime repositioning
   - E) Transfer the patient to an acute psychiatric inpatient unit for permanent long-term management of behavioral symptoms of dementia
   - **Answer:** C

241. A 73-year-old male named Harry was admitted to the hospital for 12 days following an ischemic stroke with left hemiplegia. He needs intensive physical therapy, occupational therapy, and speech therapy to regain function, along with daily skilled nursing assessment. He is not yet stable enough for home discharge. The medical team discusses post-acute placement options. Which of the following best describes the appropriate placement and its Medicare coverage?
   - A) Transfer to a skilled nursing facility (SNF) for post-acute rehabilitation and skilled nursing care; Medicare Part A covers SNF care at 100% for days 1–20 after a qualifying hospital stay of ≥3 days, with cost-sharing from days 21–100
   - B) Discharge to a long-term care (LTC) custodial nursing home; Medicare Part A covers all custodial nursing home costs permanently without any duration limit
   - C) Admission to an acute inpatient psychiatric hospital for stroke rehabilitation, as neurological events are classified as psychiatric emergencies under Medicare guidelines
   - D) Transfer to an assisted living facility (ALF) for post-stroke rehabilitation; Medicare Part A fully covers all ALF costs for up to 2 years after a qualifying hospital stay
   - E) Direct home discharge with Medicare Part A-funded 24-hour home nursing care for 6 months to complete rehabilitation
   - **Answer:** A

251. A 97-year-old female named Norma with Parkinson's disease meets nursing home level of care criteria but wishes to remain at home. She has limited income and assets and is Medicaid eligible. The social worker mentions that Norma may qualify for a program that uses Medicaid funds to purchase home-based services as an alternative to nursing home placement. Which of the following programs is being described?
   - A) The Medicaid Disproportionate Share Hospital (DSH) program, which provides financial support to hospitals serving large numbers of low-income patients but does not fund home care services
   - B) Medicaid Home and Community-Based Services (HCBS) 1915(c) waiver program, which allows states to use Medicaid funds to provide personal care aides, adult day health, respite care, home modifications, and case management as alternatives to nursing home placement
   - C) Medicare Part C (Medicare Advantage), which replaces all Medicaid benefits and provides comprehensive home care services for dually eligible beneficiaries
   - D) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit program, which provides a home health aide for 365 days per year for all homebound Medicare beneficiaries
   - E) The Consolidated Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act (COBRA), which allows individuals to continue employer-sponsored health insurance after job loss but does not cover home care services
   - **Answer:** B

261. An attorney named Louise advising a geriatric care team in Texas asks about the legal basis for providing community-based care as an alternative to institutionalization for Medicaid-eligible older adults with disabilities. Which of the following landmark legal decisions established the principle that states must provide services to people with disabilities in the most integrated, least restrictive setting appropriate to their needs?
   - A) Brown v. Board of Education (1954), a Supreme Court civil rights ruling addressing racial segregation in public schools
   - B) Roe v. Wade (1973), a Supreme Court decision related to reproductive rights that has no relevance to the provision of community-based care for older adults
   - C) Olmstead v. L.C. (1999), a U.S. Supreme Court decision interpreting Title II of the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA), which established that the unjustified institutionalization of persons with disabilities constitutes discrimination and requires states to provide community-based services when clinically appropriate and the individual chooses community placement
   - D) The Emergency Medical Treatment and Labor Act (EMTALA, 1986), which mandates emergency medical screening but does not address long-term residential placement decisions

271. A 83-year-old female named Eleanor is newly diagnosed with early Parkinson's disease and asks her clinician about financial planning for future long-term care needs. The clinician discusses long-term care insurance. Which of the following accurately describes the role and key limitations of traditional long-term care insurance in financing geriatric care?
   - A) Long-term care insurance policies universally cover 100% of all care costs indefinitely with no caps, exclusions, or waiting periods
   - B) Medicare Part A automatically serves as long-term care insurance and covers all custodial nursing home and assisted living costs for beneficiaries who need them
   - C) Long-term care insurance (LTCI) helps pay for nursing home, assisted living, and home care costs not covered by Medicare; however, premiums are expensive, most policies require medical underwriting (making purchase after diagnosis difficult or impossible), benefits may have daily caps, and only about 7–8% of older Americans currently hold LTCI policies
   - D) Long-term care insurance is a mandatory federal benefit for all Americans age 65 and older, funded through Social Security payroll taxes
   - E) Long-term care insurance can be purchased at any age, even with a pre-existing diagnosis of dementia or advanced cancer, at no additional premium cost
   - **Answer:** C

281. A 75-year-old male named Howard is a resident of a skilled nursing facility. The patient's family reports that the facility has been deciding his daily schedule, restricting his phone calls to family, denying his requests for a different roommate, and refusing to share his care plan with him. Which of the following best describes the regulatory framework protecting this resident's rights?
   - A) The Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1987 (OBRA 1987) established a comprehensive Nursing Home Resident Bill of Rights, including the right to be fully informed of one's care plan, to participate in care decisions, to maintain personal privacy, to communicate freely with family, and to voice grievances without retaliation
   - B) Nursing home residents surrender all individual rights upon admission and must fully comply with facility scheduling and care decisions without the right to any input
   - C) The only right protected under federal law for nursing home residents is the right to receive meals three times daily; all other daily life decisions are at the facility's discretion
   - D) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman program is solely responsible for writing nursing home care plans and has no advocacy or rights-protection function
   - E) Resident rights in nursing homes are governed exclusively by individual state laws, and no federal legislation provides any protections to nursing home residents
   - **Answer:** A

   - C) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman program is solely responsible for writing nursing home care plans and has no advocacy or rights-protection function
   - D) The Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1987 (OBRA 1987) established a comprehensive Nursing Home Resident Bill of Rights, including the right to be fully informed of one's care plan, to participate in care decisions, to maintain personal privacy, to communicate freely with family, and to voice grievances without retaliation
   - E) The only right protected under federal law for nursing home residents is the right to receive meals three times daily; all other daily life decisions are at the facility's discretion
   - **Answer:** D

290. A 73-year-old male named Earl is a resident of a skilled nursing facility. Which of the following best describes the regulatory framework protecting this resident's rights?

   - E) The Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1987 (OBRA 1987) established a comprehensive Nursing Home Resident Bill of Rights, including the right to be fully informed of one's care plan, to participate in care decisions, to maintain personal privacy, to communicate freely with family, and to voice grievances without retaliation
   - **Answer:** E

291. A research team led by Dr. Lillian in Florida presents a study on predictors of nursing home placement in older adults. Which of the following combinations of factors has been most consistently associated with nursing home admission in large epidemiological studies?
   - A) Advanced age alone (≥80 years) is the single strongest predictor of nursing home placement, independent of functional status or social support
   - B) High socioeconomic status is the primary predictor of nursing home placement, as wealthy individuals are more likely to choose institutional care than home care
   - C) Living in a rural geographic area is the single most important predictor of nursing home placement, regardless of the patient's functional status or caregiver availability
   - D) Cognitive impairment (especially dementia), dependence in multiple instrumental and basic ADLs, behavioral disturbances (wandering, nocturnal agitation), caregiver unavailability or burnout, urinary and fecal incontinence, and history of falls
   - E) The presence of mild hypertension and hypercholesterolemia are the most common primary triggers for nursing home placement in the United States
   - **Answer:** D

301. A 79-year-old female named Grace with heart failure, diabetes, and moderate dementia is being discharged from the hospital to a skilled nursing facility for post-acute rehabilitation. The discharge team is implementing a structured care transitions program. Which of the following is the most important element of safe hospital-to-SNF care transition to prevent early readmission?
   - A) Using verbal-only handoff communication to the SNF nursing staff to save time, as written documentation is not required by The Joint Commission
   - B) A structured medication reconciliation (accurate and complete transfer of the medication list), a detailed discharge summary transmitted to the SNF before or at the time of transfer, clear communication of pending tests and follow-up needs, and a follow-up phone call within 48–72 hours
   - C) Scheduling the patient's first post-discharge physician follow-up appointment in 3 months, as stable patients do not require early reassessment
   - D) Providing the patient with a thick discharge folder of educational pamphlets and assuming the patient will manage their own care transition independently
   - E) Instructing the SNF to restart all home medications from memory without providing a written reconciled medication list
   - **Answer:** B

311. The medical director of a skilled nursing facility in Illinois, Dr. Harry, is restructuring the facility's care model to use a comprehensive interdisciplinary team approach. Which of the following best describes the appropriate roles of each interdisciplinary team member in the long-term care setting?
   - A) Physical therapists in nursing homes provide exclusively exercise instruction classes for groups of residents and do not conduct individual patient assessments
   - B) Social workers in nursing homes are limited to billing and insurance verification tasks and have no clinical role in patient assessment or care planning
   - C) The physician alone should make all care decisions without input from nursing, social work, or therapy disciplines, as other team members have no clinical decision-making authority in nursing home settings
   - D) The interdisciplinary team includes the physician/NP/PA (medical management), registered nurse (skilled care, care plan coordination), social worker (psychosocial assessment, discharge planning, family communication), physical therapist (mobility and strength), occupational therapist (ADL function and home safety), speech-language pathologist (swallowing and communication), dietitian (nutritional assessment), and activities coordinator (recreational and cognitive engagement)
   - E) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman is a full member of the clinical care team and attends all care plan meetings as a co-treating provider
   - **Answer:** D

320. The medical director of a skilled nursing facility in New York, Dr. Mildred, is restructuring the facility's care model to use a comprehensive interdisciplinary team approach. Which of the following best describes the appropriate roles of each interdisciplinary team member in the long-term care setting?
   - A) Physical therapists in nursing homes provide exclusively exercise instruction classes for groups of residents and do not conduct individual patient assessments
   - B) The interdisciplinary team includes the physician/NP/PA (medical management), registered nurse (skilled care, care plan coordination), social worker (psychosocial assessment, discharge planning, family communication), physical therapist (mobility and strength), occupational therapist (ADL function and home safety), speech-language pathologist (swallowing and communication), dietitian (nutritional assessment), and activities coordinator (recreational and cognitive engagement)
   - C) The physician alone should make all care decisions without input from nursing, social work, or therapy disciplines, as other team members have no clinical decision-making authority in nursing home settings
   - D) Social workers in nursing homes are limited to billing and insurance verification tasks and have no clinical role in patient assessment or care planning
   - E) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman is a full member of the clinical care team and attends all care plan meetings as a co-treating provider
   - **Answer:** B

321. A family member contacts the clinic of a 71-year-old male named Bernard residing in a nursing home. They report that Bernard has been complaining of not receiving ordered meals, being left in soiled clothes for hours, and being told by staff that reporting complaints 'will make things worse.' The family is unsure how to address this with the facility. Which of the following correctly describes the role and authority of the Long-Term Care Ombudsman in this situation?
   - A) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman has authority to immediately arrest nursing home staff found to be in violation of resident care standards
   - B) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman program is a hospital-based service that exclusively resolves complaints about acute care settings and has no jurisdiction over nursing homes
   - C) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman program only accepts complaints about financial billing disputes; it has no authority to investigate issues related to care quality, dignity, or neglect
   - D) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman is a private attorney who charges hourly legal fees to represent nursing home residents in civil litigation against facilities
   - E) The Long-Term Care Ombudsman (LTCO), established by the Older Americans Act, is a state and locally-funded advocate who investigates and resolves complaints made by or on behalf of nursing home residents, with authority to access the facility, interview staff and residents, review records, and refer for regulatory action — all at no cost to the resident or family
   - **Answer:** E

331. A primary care geriatrician is implementing a standardized social determinants of health (SDOH) screening program for all older adult patients. A 87-year-old male named Kenneth screens positive for food insecurity, housing instability, lack of transportation to medical appointments, social isolation, and inability to pay utility bills. Which of the following represents the most evidence-based and comprehensive clinical response to a positive SDOH screening?
   - A) Immediately hospitalize the patient for all identified social needs, as the inpatient setting is the most efficient place to resolve food insecurity and housing instability
   - B) Prescribe a multi-vitamin and high-calorie supplement as the sole intervention for food insecurity and dismiss the patient with no further SDOH follow-up
   - C) Tell the patient that social issues are outside the scope of clinical medicine and that they should contact a charity organization independently
   - D) Refer the patient to the emergency department for all social needs, as only emergency medicine physicians are qualified to address social determinants of health
   - E) Document identified SDOH needs in the problem list; refer to a licensed clinical social worker and community health worker for individualized resource navigation; connect with the Area Agency on Aging for benefit enrollment assistance; and follow up within 30 days to assess whether connections to resources were successfully made
   - **Answer:** E

341. A 89-year-old male named Earl attends his Medicare Annual Wellness Visit (AWV). During the visit, the clinician is expected to assess the patient's health status and social needs. Which of the following accurately describes a required or recommended component of the Medicare Annual Wellness Visit that directly addresses social determinants of health and aging-related risks?
   - A) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit may only be performed in hospital outpatient settings by board-certified geriatricians and cannot be delivered by primary care physicians or nurse practitioners
   - B) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit requires a review of the patient's functional status, cognition (using a structured screen), fall risk, depression screening (PHQ-9 or equivalent), advance care planning discussion, and social history including caregiver support, living situation, and community resource needs
   - C) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit requires a full physical examination (auscultation, percussion, and palpation of all organ systems) and a complete blood count, metabolic panel, and urinalysis for all patients
   - D) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit is a one-time benefit available only to Medicare beneficiaries in the first year of Medicare enrollment and cannot be repeated annually
   - E) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit is identical to a standard sick visit and consists solely of evaluating the chief complaint with no preventive or social assessment components
   - **Answer:** B

346. A 71-year-old male named Carl attends his Medicare Annual Wellness Visit (AWV). During the visit, the clinician is expected to assess the patient's health status and social needs. Which of the following accurately describes a required or recommended component of the Medicare Annual Wellness Visit that directly addresses social determinants of health and aging-related risks?
   - A) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit requires a review of the patient's functional status, cognition (using a structured screen), fall risk, depression screening (PHQ-9 or equivalent), advance care planning discussion, and social history including caregiver support, living situation, and community resource needs
   - B) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit is a one-time benefit available only to Medicare beneficiaries in the first year of Medicare enrollment and cannot be repeated annually
   - C) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit requires a full physical examination (auscultation, percussion, and palpation of all organ systems) and a complete blood count, metabolic panel, and urinalysis for all patients
   - D) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit may only be performed in hospital outpatient settings by board-certified geriatricians and cannot be delivered by primary care physicians or nurse practitioners
   - E) The Medicare Annual Wellness Visit is identical to a standard sick visit and consists solely of evaluating the chief complaint with no preventive or social assessment components
   - **Answer:** A
